\displaystyle=-\gamma\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)^{\mathsf{T}}s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))

(37)

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}

\displaystyle=\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\left(\hat{\mathbf{v}}-\hat{\mathbf{a}}\right)

(38)

The properties of (
36
) are summarized in the following proposition.

Proposition 5.1
.

Consider the higher-order adaptation algorithm (
36
) with
\mathcal{N}(t)=1+\mu\|\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x}(t),t)\|^{2}
and
\mu>\frac{\gamma}{\eta\beta}
. Then, all trajectories
(\mathbf{x}(t),\hat{\mathbf{v}}(t),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t))
remain bounded,
s(\mathbf{x}(\cdot),\mathbf{x}_{d}(\cdot))\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty}\cap\mathcal{L}_{2}
,
\left(\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\cdot)-\hat{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot)\right)\in\mathcal{L}_{2}
,
s(\mathbf{x}(t),\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))\rightarrow 0
and
\mathbf{x}(t)\rightarrow\mathbf{x}_{d}(t)
.

The proof follows by consideration of the Lyapunov-like function

V(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},\hat{\mathbf{v}},t)=\frac{1}{2}\left(s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))^{2}+\frac{1}{\gamma}\|\tilde{\mathbf{v}}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{\gamma}\|\hat{\mathbf{v}}-\hat{\mathbf{a}}\|^{2}\right).

Remark 5.1
.

The transformation to a system of two first-order equations may seem somewhat
ad-hoc
, but it follows immediately by consideration of the non-Euclidean Bregman Lagrangian (
34
). Indeed, it is easy to check that
\hat{\mathbf{v}}=\hat{\mathbf{a}}+\frac{\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}}{\beta\mathcal{N}(t)}
, which is precisely the adaptive control equivalent of
\mathbf{x}+e^{-\overline{\alpha}(t)}\dot{\mathbf{x}}
in the first argument of
d_{\psi}\left(\cdot\ \middle\|\ \cdot\right)
in (
34
). The transformation is also readily apparent by use of the
Bregman Hamiltonian

\mathcal{H}(\hat{\mathbf{a}},\mathbf{p})=\frac{1}{2}\beta\mathcal{N}(t)e^{-\overline{\gamma}(t)}\|\mathbf{p}\|^{2}+\gamma e^{\overline{\gamma}(t)}\left[\frac{d}{dt}\frac{1}{2}s(\mathbf{x}(t),\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))^{2}\right],

(39)

which, via Hamilton’s equations, leads to

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\partial\mathcal{H}}{\partial\hat{\mathbf{a}}}=-\gamma e^{\overline{\gamma}(t)}\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)^{\mathsf{T}}s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t)),

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\mathcal{H}}{\partial\mathbf{p}}=\beta\mathcal{N}(t)e^{-\overline{\gamma}(t)}\mathbf{p}.

Defining
\hat{\mathbf{v}}=e^{-\overline{\gamma}(t)}\mathbf{p}+\hat{\mathbf{a}}
immediately leads to (
37
) & (
38
). This line of reasoning was recently investigated further by
Gaudio et al. 2021
. As is typical in classical mechanics, the Bregman Hamiltonian may be obtained from a Legendre transform of the Bregman Lagrangian. The Hamiltonian dynamics may be useful for discrete-time algorithm development through application of symplectic discretization techniques
(
Betancourt et al. 2018
;
França et al. 2019
;
Shi et al. 2019
)
.
\ \ \diamond

Remark 5.2
.

It is well known, for example from a passivity interpretation of the Lyapunov-like analysis
(
Slotine and Li 1991
)
, that the pure integrator in the standard Slotine and Li adaptation law (
8
) can be replaced by any linear positive real transfer function containing a pure integrator. The higher-order algorithms presented in this work are distinct from this approach, as most clearly seen by the state-dependent damping term in (
36
).
\ \ \diamond

Remark 5.3
.

In
Wibisono et al. 2016
, the suggested Lyapunov function in the Euclidean setting is
V(\mathbf{x},\dot{\mathbf{x}},t)=\|\mathbf{x}+e^{-\overline{\alpha}(t)}\dot{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{x}_{*}\|^{2}+e^{\overline{\beta}(t)}f(\mathbf{x})
where
\mathbf{x}_{*}
is the global optimum and
f(\mathbf{x})
is the loss function. Noting that
\hat{\mathbf{v}}
is the equivalent of
\mathbf{x}+e^{-\overline{\alpha}(t)}\dot{\mathbf{x}}
in the adaptive control context (see Remark
5.1
), we see that the Lyapunov-like function used to prove stability of the adaptive law (
36
) is similar to that used to prove convergence in the optimization context. The loss function term
f(\mathbf{x})
is replaced by
\frac{1}{2}s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))^{2}
, and it is necessary to add the additional term
\frac{1}{\gamma}\|\hat{\mathbf{v}}-\hat{\mathbf{a}}\|^{2}
.

6
Adaptation laws with momentum

In this section, we develop several new adaptation laws for both linearly and nonlinearly parameterized systems. We begin by noting that the Bregman Lagrangian generates velocity gradient algorithms with momentum, which provides a path towards adaptive control algorithms similar to Nesterov’s seminal method. We prove some general conditions under which these momentum algorithms will achieve stable convergence. By analogy with integral velocity gradient functionals, we then derive a proportional-integral scheme to implement a first-order composite adaptation law driven directly by the function approximation error. We subsequently fuse the generating functional for the composite law with the Bregman Lagrangian to construct a composite algorithm with momentum. We then derive momentum algorithms for nonlinearly parameterized systems satisfying the monotonicity requirements in Assumption
2.2
.

6.1
Velocity gradient algorithms with momentum

As noted in Section
5
, the Bregman Lagrangian (
35
) that generates the higher-order algorithm (
36
) contains the local velocity gradient functional
Q(\mathbf{x},t)=\frac{1}{2}s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))^{2}
that gives rise to the Slotine and Li controller (
8
). Based on this observation, we define local and integral higher-order velocity gradient algorithms via the Euclidean Bregman Lagrangian. We begin with the local functional

\mathcal{L}\left(\hat{\mathbf{a}},\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}},t\right)=e^{\int_{0}^{t}\beta\mathcal{N}(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\beta\mathcal{N}(t)}\left(\frac{1}{2}\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}^{\mathsf{T}}\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}-\gamma\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\frac{d}{dt}Q(\mathbf{x},t)\right),

(40)

which generates the higher-order law

\ddot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}+\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}\left(\beta\mathcal{N}(t)-\frac{\dot{\mathcal{N}}(t)}{\mathcal{N}(t)}\right)=-\gamma\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\nabla_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}\dot{Q}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t).

(41)

Algorithm (
41
) can be re-written as two first-order systems

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}

\displaystyle=-\gamma\nabla_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}\dot{Q}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t),

(42)

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}

\displaystyle=\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\left(\hat{\mathbf{v}}-\hat{\mathbf{a}}\right).

(43)

To drive
Q(\mathbf{x}(t),t)
to zero, we require the following technical assumption in addition to Assumptions
2.3
&
2.5
. This assumption replaces Assumption
2.4
for first-order velocity gradient algorithms.

Assumption 6.1
.

There exists a time-dependent signal
N(t)
and non-negative scalar values
\beta\geq 0,\mu\geq 0
such that the time-derivative of the goal functional evaluated at the true parameters,
\dot{Q}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a},t)
, satisfies the following inequality for all
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a},\hat{\mathbf{a}},\hat{\mathbf{v}}
, and
t

\dot{Q}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a},t)-\frac{\beta\mu}{\gamma}N(t)\|\hat{\mathbf{a}}-\hat{\mathbf{v}}\|^{2}+2\left(\hat{\mathbf{a}}-\hat{\mathbf{v}}\right)^{\mathsf{T}}\nabla_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}\dot{Q}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t)\leq-\rho(Q(\mathbf{x},t)).

(44)

In (
44
),
\rho(\cdot)
is positive definite, continuous in its argument, and satisfies
\rho(0)=0
.
\ \ \diamond

Assumption
6.1
is a formal statement that we may “complete the square” on the left-hand side of (
44
). For example, for
\nabla_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}\dot{Q}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t)=\mathbf{Y}^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathbf{x},t)s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))
and for
\dot{Q}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a},t)=-\eta s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))^{2}
, we may choose
N(t)=\|\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x}(t),t)^{\mathsf{T}}\|^{2}
. With Assumption
6.1
in hand, we can state the following proposition.

Proposition 6.1
.

Consider the algorithm (
41
) or its equivalent form (
42
) & (
43
), and assume
Q(\mathbf{x},t)
satisfies Assumptions
2.3
,
2.5
, and
6.1
. Then, all trajectories
(\mathbf{x}(t),\hat{\mathbf{v}}(t),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t))
remain bounded,
\left(\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\cdot)-\hat{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot)\right)\in\mathcal{L}_{2}
, and
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}Q(\mathbf{x}(t),t)=0
.

The proof is given in Appendix
F.8
. By taking
Q(\mathbf{x},t)=\frac{1}{2}s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))^{2}
in Proposition
6.1
, we immediately recover Proposition
5.1
.

We now consider the integral functional

\mathcal{L}\left(\hat{\mathbf{a}},\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}},t\right)=e^{\int_{0}^{t}\beta\mathcal{N}(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\beta\mathcal{N}(t)}\left(\frac{1}{2}\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}^{\mathsf{T}}\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}-\gamma\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\frac{d}{dt}\int_{0}^{t}R(\mathbf{x}(t^{\prime}),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t^{\prime}),t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}\right),

which generates the higher-order law

\ddot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}+\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}\left(\beta\mathcal{N}(t)-\frac{\dot{\mathcal{N}}(t)}{\mathcal{N}(t)}\right)=-\gamma\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\nabla_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}R(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t).

(45)

We again re-write (
45
) as two first-order systems

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}

\displaystyle=-\gamma\nabla_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}R(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t),

(46)

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}

\displaystyle=\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\left(\hat{\mathbf{v}}-\hat{\mathbf{a}}\right),

(47)

and now require a modified version of Assumption
6.1
.

Assumption 6.2
.

R(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t)\geq 0
for all
\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}}
, and
t
, and is uniformly continuous in
t
for bounded
\mathbf{x}
and
\hat{\mathbf{a}}
.
\nabla_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}R(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t)
is locally bounded in
\mathbf{x}
and
\hat{\mathbf{a}}
uniformly in
t
. Furthermore, there exists a time-dependent signal
N(t)
and non-negative scalar values
\beta\geq 0,\mu\geq 0
such that, for all
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a},\hat{\mathbf{a}},\hat{\mathbf{v}}
, and
t
,

R(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a},t)-R(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t)-\frac{\beta\mu}{\gamma}N(t)\|\hat{\mathbf{a}}-\hat{\mathbf{v}}\|^{2}+2\left(\hat{\mathbf{a}}-\hat{\mathbf{v}}\right)^{\mathsf{T}}\nabla_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}R(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t)\leq-kR(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t)

for some constant
k>0
.
\ \ \diamond

Similar to Assumption
6.1
, Assumption
6.2
is a formal requirement that we may “complete the square”. With slight abuse of notation, consider the case when
R(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t)=\frac{1}{2}\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},\mathbf{a},t)^{2}
. Then
R(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a},t)=0
,
\nabla_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}R(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t)=\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},\mathbf{a},t)\nabla_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}\hat{f}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t)
, and we may choose
N(t)=\|\nabla_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}\hat{f}(\mathbf{x}(t),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t),t)\|^{2}
. With Assumption
6.2
, we can state the following proposition. The proof is given in Appendix
F.9

Proposition 6.2
.

Consider algorithm (
45
) along with Assumptions
2.5
&
6.2
. Let
T_{x}
denote the maximal interval of existence of
\mathbf{x}(t)
. Then
\hat{\mathbf{v}}(t)
and
\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t)
remain bounded for
t\in[0,T_{x}]
,
\left(\hat{\mathbf{a}}-\hat{\mathbf{v}}\right)\in\mathcal{L}_{2}
over this interval, and
\int_{0}^{T_{x}}R(\mathbf{x}(t^{\prime}),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t^{\prime}),t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}<\infty
. Furthermore, for any bounded solution
\mathbf{x}(t)
, these conclusions hold for all
t
and
R(\mathbf{x}(t),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t),t)\rightarrow 0
.

As mentioned in Section
2.3
, we will be particularly interested in Proposition
6.2
when
R(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t)=\frac{1}{2}\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},\mathbf{a},t)^{2}
, which will generate composite adaptation algorithms and algorithms applicable to nonlinearly parameterized systems. Proposition
6.2
then shows that
\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x}(\cdot),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\cdot),\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}_{2}
over the interval of existence of
\mathbf{x}(t)
. As shown by Lemma
C.2
, with our error model (
6
), this is enough to show that
\mathbf{x}(t)
always remains bounded and hence
\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x}(t),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t),t)\rightarrow 0
.

Remark 6.1
.

Classically, Lyapunov-like functions used in adaptive control consist of a sum of tracking and parameter estimation error terms, with
\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}
chosen to cancel a term of unknown sign. Several Lyapunov functions in this work consist only of parameter estimation error terms, such as the Lyapunov function used in the proof of Proposition
6.2
. From a mathematical point of view, we require that
\dot{V}
is negative semi-definite and contains signals related to the tracking error. Integrating
\dot{V}
allows the application of tools from functional analysis to ensure that the control goal is accomplished. The lack of tracking error term in
V
is the origin of the additional complication that
\mathbf{x}(t)
must be shown to be bounded even after it is known that
\dot{V}\leq 0
.
\ \ \diamond

While derived via the Bregman Lagrangian, the adaptation laws presented in this section exhibit a key difference from Nesterov’s method in optimization. The gradient updates in
\dot{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}
are evaluated at
\hat{\mathbf{a}}
, while Nesterov’s method evaluates the gradient at
\hat{\mathbf{v}}
. In adaptive control, this forward prediction does not appear to be possible: in the linearly parameterized case, for example,
\nabla_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}\dot{Q}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t)=\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)^{\mathsf{T}}s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))
is independent of
\hat{\mathbf{a}}
.

6.2
First- and second-order composite adaptation laws

Here we consider the linearly parameterized setting
f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a},t)=\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)\mathbf{a}
, and derive new first- and second-order composite adaptation laws based on Propositions
2.2
&
6.2
. Composite adaptation laws are driven by two sources of error: the tracking error itself, as summarized by
s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))
in the Slotine and Li controller, and a prediction error. The prediction error term is generally obtained from an algebraic relation constructed by filtering the dynamics
(
Slotine and Li 1991
)
. We now present a composite algorithm that does not require any explicit filtering of the dynamics, but is instead driven simultaneously by
s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))
and
\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},\mathbf{a},t)
.

A starting point is to consider a hybrid local and integral velocity gradient functional

Q(\mathbf{x},t)=\frac{\gamma}{2}s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))^{2}+\frac{\kappa}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{f}^{2}(\mathbf{x}(t^{\prime}),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t^{\prime}),\mathbf{a},t^{\prime})dt^{\prime},

(48)

where
\kappa>0
and
\gamma>0
are positive learning rates weighting the contributions of each term. As discussed in Section
2.3
, the first term leads to the Slotine and Li controller. The second can be clearly seen to satisfy Assumptions
2.6
and
2.7
with
\mu(t)=0
. It also satisfies Assumption
2.5
, as
\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},\mathbf{a},t)^{2}
is a quadratic function of
\tilde{\mathbf{a}}
for linear
\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},\mathbf{a},t)
. Following the velocity gradient formalism, the resulting adaptation law is given by

\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}=-\mathbf{P}\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\gamma s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))+\kappa\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)\tilde{\mathbf{a}}\right).

(49)

Equation (
49
) depends on the function approximation error
\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},\mathbf{a},t)=Y(\mathbf{x},t)\tilde{\mathbf{a}}
, which is not measured and hence cannot be used directly in an adaptation law. Nevertheless, it can be obtained through a proportional-integral form for
\hat{\mathbf{a}}
in an identical manner to Section
2.2
. To do so, we define

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}(\mathbf{x},t)

\displaystyle=-\kappa\mathbf{P}s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)^{\mathsf{T}},

(50)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\rho}(\mathbf{x},t)

\displaystyle=\kappa\mathbf{P}\int_{x_{n}(t_{0})}^{x_{n}(t)}s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))\frac{\partial\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)^{\mathsf{T}}}{\partial x_{n}}dx_{n},

(51)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{a}}

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathbf{a}}+\boldsymbol{\xi}(\mathbf{x},t)+\boldsymbol{\rho}(\mathbf{x},t),

(52)

\displaystyle\dot{\overline{\mathbf{a}}}

\displaystyle=-\left(\kappa\eta+\gamma\right)s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))\mathbf{P}\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)^{\mathsf{T}}+\kappa s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\mathbf{P}\frac{\partial\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)}{\partial x_{i}}\dot{x}_{i}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{\rho}(\mathbf{x},t)}{\partial x_{i}}\dot{x}_{i}-\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{\xi}(\mathbf{x},t)}{\partial t}-\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{\rho}(\mathbf{x},t)}{\partial t}.

(53)

Computing
\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}
demonstrates that (
49
) is obtained through only the known signals contained in (
50
)-(
6.2
) despite its dependence on
\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)\tilde{\mathbf{a}}
.

Remark 6.2
.

The
\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)\tilde{\mathbf{a}}
term may also be obtained by following the Immersion and Invariance formalism
(
Astolfi and Ortega 2003
;
Liu et al. 2010
)
. To our knowledge, this discussion is the first that demonstrates the possibility of using a PI law in combination with a standard Lyapunov-stability motivated adaptation law to obtain a composite law.
\ \ \diamond

Remark 6.3
.

Rearranging (
49
) shows that
\dot{\tilde{\mathbf{a}}}+\mathbf{P}\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)\tilde{\mathbf{a}}=-\mathbf{P}\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)^{\mathsf{T}}s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))
, so that the additional term can be seen to add a damping term that smooths adaptation
(
Slotine and Li 1991
)
.
\ \ \diamond

Remark 6.4
.

As mentioned in Section
2.1
, for clarity of presentation we have restricted our discussion to the
n^{\text{th}}
-order system (
1
). The PI form (
52
) leads to
undesired
unknown terms in addition to the
desired
unknown term. Here, the desired unknown term is
-\kappa\mathbf{P}\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)\tilde{\mathbf{a}}
while the undesired unknown term is
-\kappa\mathbf{P}\frac{\partial\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)}{\partial x_{n}}\dot{x}_{n}s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))
. The purpose of introducing the additional proportional term
\boldsymbol{\rho}(\mathbf{x},t)
in (
50
) is to cancel this undesired unknown term. In general, cancellation of the undesired terms can be obtained by choosing
\boldsymbol{\rho}
to solve a PDE, and solutions to this PDE will only exist if the undesired term is the gradient of an auxiliary function.
\boldsymbol{\rho}
is then chosen to be exactly this auxiliary function. In some cases, the PDE can be avoided, such as through dynamic scaling techniques
(
Karagiannis et al. 2009
)
or the related embedding technique of
Tyukin 2011
.
\ \ \diamond

The properties of the adaptive law (
49
) may be summarized with the following proposition.

Proposition 6.3
.

Consider the adaptation algorithm (
49
) with a linearly parameterized unknown,
f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a},t)=\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)\mathbf{a}
. Then all trajectories
(\mathbf{x}(t),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t))
remain bounded,
s(\mathbf{x}(\cdot),\mathbf{x}_{d}(\cdot))\in\mathcal{L}_{2}\cap\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
,
\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x}(\cdot),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\cdot),\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}_{2}
,
s(\mathbf{x}(t),\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))\rightarrow 0
, and
\mathbf{x}(t)\rightarrow\mathbf{x}_{d}(t)
.

The proof is given in Appendix
F.10
.

Following the velocity gradient with momentum approach of Section
6.1
, we now obtain a higher-order composite algorithm, and give a PI implementation. We again consider a hybrid local and integral velocity gradient functional, so that (
34
) takes the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle\left(\hat{\mathbf{a}},\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}},t\right)=

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\frac{e^{\int_{0}^{t}\beta\mathcal{N}(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}}}{\beta\mathcal{N}(t)}\left(\frac{1}{2}\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}^{\mathsf{T}}\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}-\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\frac{d}{dt}\left[\frac{\gamma}{2}s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))^{2}+\frac{\kappa}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{f}^{2}(\mathbf{x}(t^{\prime}),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t^{\prime}),\mathbf{a},t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}\right]\right)

(54)

where
\gamma>0
and
\kappa>0
are positive constants weighting the two error terms. The Euler-Lagrange equations then lead to the higher-order composite system

\ddot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}+\left(\beta\mathcal{N}(t)-\frac{\dot{\mathcal{N}}(t)}{\mathcal{N}(t)}\right)\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}=-\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\gamma s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))+\kappa\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)\tilde{\mathbf{a}}\right).

(55)

As in Section
5
, (
55
) may be implemented as two first-order systems

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\gamma s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))+\kappa\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)\tilde{\mathbf{a}}\right),

(56)

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}

\displaystyle=\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\left(\hat{\mathbf{v}}-\hat{\mathbf{a}}\right).

(57)

In an implementation, (
56
) is obtained through the PI form
\hat{\mathbf{v}}=\overline{\mathbf{v}}+\boldsymbol{\xi}(\mathbf{x},t)+\boldsymbol{\rho}(\mathbf{x},t)
with
\boldsymbol{\xi}
,
\boldsymbol{\rho}
, and
\dot{\overline{\mathbf{v}}}
given by (
50
), (
51
), and (
6.2
) respectively with
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{I}
. The properties of the higher-order composite adaptation law (
55
) are stated in the following proposition.

Proposition 6.4
.

Consider the higher-order composite adaptation algorithm (
55
) for a linearly parameterized unknown,
f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a},t)=\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)\mathbf{a}
. Set
\mathcal{N}(t)=1+\mu\|\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x}(t),t)\|^{2}
and
\mu>\frac{\gamma}{\beta}\left(\frac{1}{\eta}+\frac{\kappa}{\gamma}\right)
. Then all trajectories
(\mathbf{x}(t),\hat{\mathbf{v}}(t),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t))
remain bounded,
\hat{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot)-\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}_{2}
,
s(\mathbf{x}(\cdot),\mathbf{x}_{d}(\cdot))\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty}\cap\mathcal{L}_{2}
,
\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x}(\cdot),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\cdot),\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty}\cap\mathcal{L}_{2}
,
s(\mathbf{x}(t),\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))\rightarrow 0
, and
\mathbf{x}(t)\rightarrow\mathbf{x}_{d}(t)
.

The proof is given in Appendix
F.11
.

Remark 6.5
.

The signal
\mathcal{N}(t)
may be chosen alternatively to be matrix-valued as
\mathbf{N}=\mathbf{I}+\mu\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x}(t),t)^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x}(t),t)
.
\ \ \diamond

Remark 6.6
.

The
\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)\tilde{\mathbf{a}}
term may be used in isolation, with proof by consideration of the Lyapunov-like function
V(\hat{\mathbf{a}},\hat{\mathbf{v}})=\frac{1}{2}\|\tilde{\mathbf{v}}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\hat{\mathbf{a}}-\hat{\mathbf{v}}\|^{2}
.
\ \ \diamond

Remark 6.7
.

A gain matrix
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}^{\mathsf{T}}>0
of appropriate dimension may be placed in front of
\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)^{\mathsf{T}}
in
\dot{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}
. The quadratic parameter estimation error terms in the Lyapunov function should then be replaced by the weighted terms
\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\mathbf{v}}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{P}^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{v}}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\hat{\mathbf{a}}-\hat{\mathbf{v}}\right)^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{P}^{-1}\left(\hat{\mathbf{a}}-\hat{\mathbf{v}}\right)
, and bounds on
\mu
will be given in terms of
\|\mathbf{P}\|
.
\ \ \diamond

6.3
A momentum algorithm for nonlinearly parameterized adaptive control

We now use the development in Proposition
6.2
to present a momentum algorithm applicable when the unknown parameters appear nonlinearly in the dynamics. An analogy to methods for learning generalized linear models in statistics and machine learning is explored in Appendix
D
.

We study the momentum-like variant of (
12
)

\ddot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}+\left(\beta\mathcal{N}(t)-\frac{\dot{\mathcal{N}}(t)}{\mathcal{N}(t)}\right)\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}=-\gamma\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},\mathbf{a},t)\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\mathbf{x},t),

(58)

which, as before, admits an equivalent representation in terms of two first-order systems,

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}

\displaystyle=-\gamma\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},\mathbf{a},t)\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\mathbf{x},t),

(59)

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}

\displaystyle=\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\left(\hat{\mathbf{v}}-\hat{\mathbf{a}}\right).

(60)

(
58
) may be implemented through (
59
) & (
60
) via the PI form (
13
)-(
16
) applied to the
\hat{\mathbf{v}}
variable. (
58
) may be obtained via the Bregman Lagrangian (
54
) for velocity gradient laws with momentum by choosing only the integral term. It is then necessary to modify the resulting Euler-Lagrange equations by setting
f_{m}^{\prime}
to
\pm 1
based on monotonicity of
\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},\mathbf{a},t)
as described in Section
2.2
. The following proposition summarizes the properties of (
59
) and (
60
).

Proposition 6.5
.

Consider the algorithm (
58
) or its equivalent form (
59
) & (
60
) under Assumption
2.2
with
\mathcal{N}(t)=1+\mu\|\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\mathbf{x}(t),t)\|^{2}
and
\mu>\frac{\gamma D_{1}}{\beta}
. Then, all trajectories
(\mathbf{x}(t),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t),\hat{\mathbf{v}}(t))
remain bounded,
\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x}(\cdot),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\cdot),\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}_{2}
,
\left(\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\cdot)-\hat{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot)\right)\in\mathcal{L}_{2}
,
s(\mathbf{x}(\cdot),\mathbf{x}_{d}(\cdot))\in\mathcal{L}_{2}\cap\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
,
s(\mathbf{x}(t),\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))\rightarrow 0
and
\mathbf{x}(t)\rightarrow\mathbf{x}_{d}(t)
.

The proof is given in Appendix
F.12
.

Remark 6.8
.

Remarks
6.5
&
6.7
also apply in this setting. By following the proof of Proposition
6.5
, one may also take
\mathcal{N}(t)
to be matrix-valued as
\mathbf{N}(t)=1+\mu\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\mathbf{x}(t),t)\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\mathbf{x}(t),t)^{\mathsf{T}}
. One may also use a gain matrix
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}^{\mathsf{T}}>0
of appropriate dimension.
\ \ \diamond

7
The elastic modification

As noted in Section
6.1
, one prominent difference between Nesterov’s accelerated method and the momentum algorithms presented in this work is the point at which the gradient is evaluated. This distinction suggests an alternative interpretation of the momentum algorithms in Section
6.1
as adding an additional averaging step that consists of passing the standard first-order parameter estimates through a low-pass filter in
series
. It is well-known that iterate averaging schemes for stochastic gradient descent – such as Polyak-Ruppert averaging
(
Polyak and Juditsky 1992a
;
Ruppert 1988
)
and Polyak-Juditsky averaging
(
Polyak and Juditsky 1992b
)
– can improve convergence rates via variance reduction. The momentum algorithms of Section
6.1
provide a stable way to perform iterate averaging in nonlinear adaptive control with an exponentially weighted average similar to
Neu and Rosasco 2018
, and show that the averaging rate must depend on the norm of a system signal such as
\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)
for stability.

An alternative option to perform iterate averaging would be to couple the parameter estimates to the output of a filter of the parameter estimates in
feedback
. Incorporating this type of feedback coupling is similar in spirit to the elastic averaging SGD algorithm of
Zhang et al. 2014
. Here we show that adding such feedback coupling can always be done for velocity gradient algorithms and velocity gradient algorithms with momentum. We focus on local velocity gradient algorithms: extensions to integral functionals and nonlinearly parameterized dynamics are straightforward combining the proof techniques here with those of Proposition
6.2
. Similar to iterate averaging methods in optimization, this approach may improve convergence in settings with sparse, noisy observations, such as real-world applications of the prediction methods developed in Section
4
.

Proposition 7.1
.

Consider the adaptive control algorithm

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{P}\nabla_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}\dot{Q}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t)+k\left(\overline{\mathbf{a}}-\hat{\mathbf{a}}\right),

\displaystyle\dot{\overline{\mathbf{a}}}

\displaystyle=k\left(\hat{\mathbf{a}}-\overline{\mathbf{a}}\right).

under Assumptions
2.3
-
2.5
for
k\geq 0
and
\mathbf{P}>0
. Then all solutions
(\mathbf{x}(t),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t),\overline{\mathbf{a}}(t))
remain bounded,
\left(\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\cdot)-\overline{\mathbf{a}}(\cdot)\right)\in\mathcal{L}_{2}
, and for all
\mathbf{x}(0)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}Q(\mathbf{x}(t),t)=0.

The proof is given in Appendix
F.13
. We may also state a similar result with momentum, allowing for coupling to a filtered version of both
\hat{\mathbf{v}}
and
\hat{\mathbf{a}}
.

Proposition 7.2
.

Consider the adaptive control algorithm

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{P}\nabla_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}\dot{Q}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t)+k_{v}\left(\overline{\mathbf{v}}-\hat{\mathbf{v}}\right),

\displaystyle\dot{\overline{\mathbf{v}}}

\displaystyle=k_{v}\left(\hat{\mathbf{a}}-\overline{\mathbf{a}}\right),

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}

\displaystyle=\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\left(\hat{\mathbf{v}}-\hat{\mathbf{a}}\right)+k_{a}\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\left(\overline{\mathbf{a}}-\hat{\mathbf{a}}\right)

\displaystyle\dot{\overline{\mathbf{a}}}

\displaystyle=k_{a}\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\left(\hat{\mathbf{a}}-\overline{\mathbf{a}}\right)

under Assumptions
2.3
-
2.5
. Fix
k_{v}\geq 0,k_{a}\geq 0,\gamma>0,
and
\beta\geq 2k_{v}
. Then all solutions
(\mathbf{x}(t),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t),\overline{\mathbf{a}}(t),\hat{\mathbf{v}}(t),\overline{\mathbf{v}}(t))
remain bounded,
\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\cdot)-\overline{\mathbf{a}}(\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}_{2}
,
\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\cdot)-\hat{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}_{2}
,
\hat{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot)-\overline{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}_{2}
, and for all
\mathbf{x}(0)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}Q(\mathbf{x}(t),t)=0.

The proof is given in Appendix
F.14
.

8
Natural momentum algorithms

The Bregman Lagrangian allows for the introduction of non-Euclidean metrics. In Section
5
, we took the potential function
\psi
to be the Euclidean norm,
\psi(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{x}\|^{2}
. We now show that taking
\psi
to be an arbitrary strongly convex function leads to a more general class of algorithms that can be seen as the higher-order variants of those discussed in Sections
2.4
and
3
. With the same definitions of
\bar{\alpha},\bar{\gamma}
, and
\bar{\beta}
as in Section
5
, but now taking
\psi
to be arbitrary in
d_{\psi}\left(\cdot\ \middle\|\ \cdot\right)
, the Bregman Lagrangian (
40
) takes the form

\mathcal{L}(\hat{\mathbf{a}},\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}},t)=e^{\int_{0}^{t}\beta\mathcal{N}(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}}\left(\beta\mathcal{N}(t)d_{\psi}\left(\hat{\mathbf{a}}+\frac{\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}}{\beta\mathcal{N}(t)}\ \middle\|\ \hat{\mathbf{a}}\right)-\gamma\frac{d}{dt}Q(\mathbf{x},t)\right),

(61)

with a similar expression for the integral variant. In (
61
),
d_{\psi}\left(\cdot\ \middle\|\ \cdot\right)
has its first argument evaluated at
\hat{\mathbf{a}}+\frac{\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}}{\beta\mathcal{N}(t)}
. This quantity is precisely
\hat{\mathbf{v}}
, which leads to a particularly simple form of first-order equations, as highlighted in the following propositions.

We start with the case of a local functional, which requires a modification of Assumption
6.1
.

Assumption 8.1
.

There exists a time-dependent signal
N(t)
and non-negative scalar values
\beta\geq 0,\mu\geq 0
such that the time-derivative of the goal functional evaluated at the true parameters,
\dot{Q}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a},t)
, satisfies the following inequality

\dot{Q}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a},t)-\frac{\beta\mu}{\gamma}N(t)\|\hat{\mathbf{a}}-\hat{\mathbf{v}}\|^{2}+\left(\hat{\mathbf{a}}-\hat{\mathbf{v}}\right)^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\mathbf{I}+\left[\nabla^{2}\psi(\hat{\mathbf{v}})\right]^{-1}\right)\nabla_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}\dot{Q}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t)\leq-\rho(Q(\mathbf{x},t)).

(62)

In (
62
),
\rho(\cdot)
is positive definite, continuous in its argument, and satisfies
\rho(0)=0
.
\ \ \diamond

Assumption
8.1
leads to the following non-Euclidean equivalent of Proposition
6.1
.

Proposition 8.1
.

Consider the adaptive control algorithm

\ddot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}+\left(\beta\mathcal{N}(t)-\frac{\dot{\mathcal{N}}(t)}{\mathcal{N}(t)}\right)\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}+\gamma\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\nabla^{2}\psi\left(\hat{\mathbf{a}}+\frac{\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}}{\beta\mathcal{N}(t)}\right)^{-1}\nabla_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}\dot{Q}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t)=0

(63)

or its equivalent first order form

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}

\displaystyle=-\gamma\left[\nabla^{2}\psi(\hat{\mathbf{v}})\right]^{-1}\nabla_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}\dot{Q}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t),

(64)

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}

\displaystyle=\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\left(\hat{\mathbf{v}}-\hat{\mathbf{a}}\right),

(65)

and assume
Q(\mathbf{x},t)
satisfies Assumptions
2.3
,
2.5
, and
8.1
. Then all solutions
(\mathbf{x}(t),\hat{\mathbf{v}}(t),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t))
remain bounded,
\left(\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\cdot)-\hat{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot)\right)\in\mathcal{L}_{2}
, and
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}Q(\mathbf{x}(t),t)=0
.

The proof is given in Appendix
F.15
. A particularly satisfying property of the above algorithm is that taking the
\beta\rightarrow\infty
limit recovers the first-order non-Euclidean velocity gradient algorithm.

For the integral variant, we require a modified version of Assumption
6.2
.

Assumption 8.2
.

R(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t)\geq 0
for all
\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}}
, and
t
, and is uniformly continuous in
t
for bounded
\mathbf{x}
and
\hat{\mathbf{a}}
.
\nabla_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}R(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t)
is locally bounded in
\mathbf{x}
and
\hat{\mathbf{a}}
uniformly in
t
. Furthermore, there exists a time-dependent signal
N(t)
and non-negative scalar values
\beta\geq 0,\mu\geq 0
such that, for all
\hat{\mathbf{a}}
and
\hat{\mathbf{v}}
and for some constant
k>0
,

R(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a},t)-R(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t)-\frac{\beta\mu}{\gamma}N(t)\|\hat{\mathbf{a}}-\hat{\mathbf{v}}\|^{2}+\left(\hat{\mathbf{a}}-\hat{\mathbf{v}}\right)^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\mathbf{I}+\left[\nabla^{2}\psi\left(\hat{\mathbf{v}}\right)\right]^{-1}\right)\nabla_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}R(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t)\leq-kR(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t).\ \ \diamond

With Assumption
8.2
, we can state the following proposition.

Proposition 8.2
.

Consider the algorithm

\ddot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}+\left(\beta\mathcal{N}(t)-\frac{\dot{\mathcal{N}}(t)}{\mathcal{N}(t)}\right)\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}+\gamma\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\left[\nabla^{2}\psi\left(\hat{\mathbf{a}}+\frac{\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}}{\beta\mathcal{N}(t)}\right)\right]^{-1}\nabla_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}R(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t)=0

or its equivalent first-order form

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}

\displaystyle=-\gamma\left[\nabla^{2}\psi(\hat{\mathbf{v}})\right]^{-1}\nabla_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}R(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t),

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}

\displaystyle=\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\left(\hat{\mathbf{v}}-\hat{\mathbf{a}}\right),

along with Assumptions
2.5
&
8.2
. Let
T_{x}
denote the maximal interval of existence of
\mathbf{x}(t)
. Then,
\hat{\mathbf{v}}(t)
and
\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t)
remain bounded for
t\in[0,T_{x})
and
\left(\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\cdot)-\hat{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot)\right)\in\mathcal{L}_{2}
over this interval. Moreover,
\int_{0}^{T_{x}}R(\mathbf{x}(t^{\prime}),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t^{\prime}),t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}<\infty
. For any bounded solution
\mathbf{x}(t)
, these conclusions hold for all
t
and
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}R(\mathbf{x}(t),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t),t)=0
.

The proof is given in Appendix
F.16
. Propositions
8.1
&
8.2
may be applied to derive non-Euclidean variants of the non-filtered composite algorithm and the momentum algorithm for nonlinearly parameterized adaptive control presented in this work
6
6

6

The nonlinearly parameterized algorithm requires slight modification, accounting for monotonicity.
. As a concrete example, these general theorems immediately lead to stable adaptation laws for the
n^{\text{th}}
order system (
1
) in the linearly parameterized setting given by

\ddot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}+\left(\beta\mathcal{N}(t)-\frac{\dot{\mathcal{N}}(t)}{\mathcal{N}(t)}\right)\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}+\gamma\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\left(\nabla^{2}\psi\left(\hat{\mathbf{a}}+\frac{\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}}{\beta\mathcal{N}(t)}\right)\right)^{-1}\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)^{\mathsf{T}}s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))=0.

or the equivalent system of equations

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}

\displaystyle=-\gamma\left(\nabla^{2}\psi\left(\hat{\mathbf{v}}\right)\right)^{-1}\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)^{\mathsf{T}}s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t)),

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}

\displaystyle=\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\left(\hat{\mathbf{v}}-\hat{\mathbf{a}}\right).

Remark 8.1
.

There are several alternative forms of natural adaptive algorithms with momentum that can also be proven to converge. For example, with similar techniques, it can be shown that the algorithm

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}

\displaystyle=-\gamma\left(\nabla^{2}\psi(\hat{\mathbf{v}})\right)^{-1}\nabla_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}\dot{Q}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t),

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}

\displaystyle=\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\left(\nabla^{2}\psi(\hat{\mathbf{a}})\right)^{-1}\left(\nabla\psi(\hat{\mathbf{v}})-\nabla\psi(\hat{\mathbf{a}})\right),

is also globally convergent. This algorithm is equivalent to performing the Euclidean variant entirely in the mirrored domain; it can be be re-written

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\nabla\psi(\hat{\mathbf{v}})

\displaystyle=-\gamma\nabla_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}\dot{Q}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t),

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\nabla\psi(\hat{\mathbf{a}})

\displaystyle=\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\left(\nabla\psi(\hat{\mathbf{v}})-\nabla\psi(\hat{\mathbf{a}})\right),

which shows that
\nabla\psi(\hat{\mathbf{a}})
obtains the same values over time as
\hat{\mathbf{a}}
computed via algorithm (
41
). A second modification with
\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}
driven by
\hat{\mathbf{v}}
rather than
\nabla\psi(\hat{\mathbf{v}})
is given by

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}

\displaystyle=-\gamma\left(\nabla^{2}\psi(\hat{\mathbf{v}})\right)^{-1}\nabla_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}\dot{Q}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t),

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}

\displaystyle=\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\left(\nabla^{2}\psi(\hat{\mathbf{a}})\right)^{-1}\left(\hat{\mathbf{v}}-\hat{\mathbf{a}}\right).\ \ \diamond

Remark 8.2
.

Discretization of the
\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}
and
\dot{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}
dynamics directly results in a natural gradient-like update
(
Amari 1998
)
, while discretization of the
\frac{d}{dt}\nabla\psi(\hat{\mathbf{a}})
and
\frac{d}{dt}\nabla\psi(\hat{\mathbf{v}})
dynamics leads to a mirror descent-like update
(
Beck and Teboulle 2003
;
Nemirovski and Yudin 1983
)
; these discrete-time algorithms have the same continuous-time limit
(
Krichene et al. 2015
)
.
\ \ \diamond

Remark 8.3
.

The implicit regularization properties of the first-order algorithms considered in Section
3
extend to the higher-order setting captured by algorithm (
63
). The assumption that
\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t)\rightarrow\hat{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty}
can be used to conclude
\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}(t)\rightarrow 0
. As noted in Section
5
,
\hat{\mathbf{v}}(t)=\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t)+\frac{\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}(t)}{\beta\mathcal{N}(t)}
, and this shows that
\hat{\mathbf{v}}(t)\rightarrow\hat{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty}
. Because
\dot{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}
in (
64
) is identical to algorithm (
42
), the result follows. A formal statement of this fact is provided in Appendix
G.1
\ \ \diamond

9
Simulations

In this section, we perform several numerical experiments demonstrating the validity of our theory, and consider a number of applications of our non-Euclidean adaptive laws.

9.1
Convergence and implicit regularization of a momentum algorithm for nonlinearly parameterized systems

We first empirically verify the global convergence and implicit regularization of the momentum algorithm for nonlinearly parameterized systems (
58
). In particular, we consider a second order system

\displaystyle\dot{x}_{1}

\displaystyle=x_{2},

\displaystyle\dot{x}_{2}

\displaystyle=u(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},t)-f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a}),

with an unknown system dynamics of the form

f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a})=\sigma\left(\tanh\left(\mathbf{V}\mathbf{x}\right)^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{a}\right).

(66)

Equation (
66
) represents a three-layer neural network with input layer
\mathbf{x}
, hidden layer weights
\mathbf{V}
, hidden layer nonlinearity
\tanh(\cdot)
, hidden layer weights
\mathbf{a}
, and output nonlinearity
\sigma(x)=e^{.1x}
. The system model (
66
) can clearly be seen to satisfy Assumption
2.2
with
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\mathbf{x})=\tanh(\mathbf{V}\mathbf{x})
7
7

7

While the exponential is not globally Lipschitz continuous, it is locally.
. We apply the non-Euclidean variant of algorithm (
58
) with
\psi(\cdot)=\frac{1}{2}\|\cdot\|_{p}^{2}
with
p\in\{1.1,2,4,6,10\}
where
p=1.1
represents an approximation to the
\ell_{1}
norm and
p=2
represents the Euclidean variant. Further simulation details, including an implementation of the algorithm in PI form, are provided in Appendix
A.1
.

The tracking error for each choice of
\psi
along with a baseline comparison to fixed
\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t)=\hat{\mathbf{a}}(0)
is shown in Figure
1
A. Figures
1
B-F show trajectories for
100
out of the
500
parameters. The timescale on each axis is set to show the trajectories approximately until the parameters converge for the given algorithm. Each case results in remarkably different dynamics and resulting converged parameter vectors
\hat{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty}^{\psi}
. The tracking performance is good for each algorithm.

Further insight can be gained into the structure of the parameter vector
\hat{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty}^{\psi}
found by each adaptation algorithm by consideration of the histograms (rug plots shown on
x
axis) for
\hat{\mathbf{a}}
at the end of the simulation in Figures
2
A-F. Figure
2
A shows the true parameter vector. The choice of
\psi(\cdot)=\frac{1}{2}\|\cdot\|_{1.1}^{2}
in Figure
2
B leads to a sparse solution with most of the weight placed on a few parameters. This is consistent with
\ell_{1}
regularized solutions found by the LASSO algorithm
(
Tibshirani 1996
)
. The inset displays a closer view around zero. The choice of
\psi(\cdot)=\frac{1}{2}\|\cdot\|_{2}^{2}
in Figure
2
C (Euclidean adaptation law) leads to a parameter vector
\hat{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}\|\cdot\|_{2}^{2}}\neq\mathbf{a}
that is roughly Gaussian distributed. This distribution highlights the implicit
\ell_{2}
regularization of standard adaptation laws. The progression from
\psi(\cdot)=\frac{1}{2}\|\cdot\|_{4}^{2}
to
\psi(\cdot)=\frac{1}{2}\|\cdot\|_{10}^{2}
displays a trend towards approximate
\ell_{\infty}
-norm regularization: the distribution of parameters is pushed to be bimodal and peaked around
\pm 1
, and the
\ell_{\infty}
norm of
\hat{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty}
decreases as
p
is increased.

Figure
3
A shows the function approximation error
\tilde{f}^{2}(\mathbf{x}(t),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t),\mathbf{a})
for each algorithm along with a reference value for fixed
\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t)=\hat{\mathbf{a}}(0)
. Each algorithm, as expected by our theory and seen by the low tracking error in Figure
1
A, pushes
\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x}(t),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t),\mathbf{a})^{2}
to zero despite the different forms of regularization imposed on the parameter vectors. Figures
3
B-F show the control input as a function of time along with the unique “ideal” control law
u(t)=\ddot{x}_{d}(t)+f(\mathbf{x}_{d}(t),\mathbf{a})
valid when
\mathbf{x}(0)=\mathbf{x}_{d}(0)
. All control inputs can be seen to converge to the ideal law, though the rate of convergence depends on the choice of algorithm. The control input is of reasonable magnitude throughout adaptation for each algorithm.

9.2
Learning to control with primitives

It is well-known that mirror descent with respect to the entropy
\psi\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right)=\sum_{i}\hat{\beta}_{i}\log\hat{\beta}_{i}
can improve the dimension-dependence of convergence rates in comparison to projected gradient descent when optimizing over the simplex
\Theta=\left\{\hat{\beta}_{i}:\hat{\beta}_{i}\geq 0,\>\>\>\sum_{i}\hat{\beta}_{i}=1\right\}

(
Hazan 2016
)
. Here we demonstrate that the same phenomenon appears in adaptive control.

As a model problem for this setting, we consider the second-order system

\displaystyle\dot{x}_{1}

\displaystyle=x_{2},

\displaystyle\dot{x}_{2}

\displaystyle=u(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}})-\tanh\left(\mathbf{V}\mathbf{x}\right)^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{a},

with
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
a fixed vector of unknown parameters and
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times 2}
a random matrix with
V_{ij}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\frac{1}{p^{2}}\right)
. We assume access to control primitives
\left\{u_{i}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}}^{(i)})\right\}_{i=1}^{N}
capable of tracking desired trajectories
\left\{x_{d}^{(i)}\right\}_{i=1}^{N}
, and we consider tracking a desired trajectory
x_{d}(t)
given piecewise by randomly drawn
x_{d}^{(i)}
. Details on the definition of the individual desired trajectory, the control inputs
u_{i}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}}^{(i)})
, and the randomly specified desired trajectory used for the comparison can be found in Appendix
A.2
.

To leverage the available control primitives, we use the input

u\left(\mathbf{x},\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\left\{\hat{\mathbf{a}}^{(i)}\right\}_{i=1}^{N}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\hat{\beta}_{i}u_{i}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}}^{(i)})=\mathbf{u}\left(\mathbf{x},\left\{\hat{\mathbf{a}}^{(i)}\right\}_{i=1}^{N}\right)\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

Above,
\mathbf{u}\left(\mathbf{x},\left\{\hat{\mathbf{a}}^{(i)}\right\}_{i=1}^{N}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times N}
is a row vector with components
u_{i}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}}^{(i)})
.

We consider two adaptation laws,

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}=-\gamma\mathbf{u}\left(\mathbf{x},\left\{\hat{\mathbf{a}}^{(i)}\right\}_{i=1}^{N}\right)^{\mathsf{T}}s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t)),

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\nabla\psi\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right)=-\gamma\mathbf{u}\left(\mathbf{x},\left\{\hat{\mathbf{a}}^{(i)}\right\}_{i=1}^{N}\right)^{\mathsf{T}}s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t)),

with projection of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
onto the simplex. In both cases, we initialize
\hat{\beta}_{i}=\frac{1}{N}
and set
\gamma=.25
.

The results are shown in Figure
4
. Figure
4
A shows convergence of
s(\mathbf{x}(t),\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))
for both adaptive laws.
s(\mathbf{x}(t),\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))
jumps every
200
units of time as the task changes discretely. While both converge, adaptation with respect to the entropy converges significantly faster and minimizes
s(\mathbf{x}(t),\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))
to lower values. This effect is more prominently displayed in Figure
4
B, which shows convergence of
s(\mathbf{x}(t),\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))
on a logarithmic scale. Figures
4
C and D show the parameter trajectories for the mirror descent and Euclidean laws, respectively. The mirror descent law displays trajectories in which fewer parameters stray from zero. Those that do drift from zero stray an order of magnitude further than the Euclidean law. The discrete changes of the desired trajectory are more visible in the parameter trajectories for the mirror descent law, indicating a faster reaction to the task.

\begin{overpic}[entropy_tuned_opacity/s.pdf]
\put(5.0,60.0){{A}}
\end{overpic}

\begin{overpic}[entropy_tuned_opacity/s_log.pdf]
\put(5.0,60.0){{B}}
\end{overpic}

\begin{overpic}[entropy_tuned_opacity/params_md.pdf]
\put(5.0,60.0){{C}}
\end{overpic}

\begin{overpic}[entropy_tuned_opacity/params_sl.pdf]
\put(5.0,60.0){{D}}
\end{overpic}

Figure 4
:
Learning to control with primitives.
(A)
s
on a linear scale for the Euclidean and mirror descent-like adaptation laws. Mirror descent converges faster for all tasks. (B)
s
on a logarithmic scale for the Euclidean and mirror descent-like adaptation laws. Mirror descent converges faster and minimizes
s
further for all tasks. (C/D) Parameter trajectories for the mirror descent and Euclidean adaptation laws. Mirror descent leads to smoother trajectories with fewer parameters straying from
0
.

9.3
Dynamics prediction for Hamiltonian systems

Similar to
Chen et al. 2019
, consider the Hamiltonian for three point masses interacting in
d=2
dimensions via Newtonian gravitation (in units such that the gravitational constant
G=1
),

\mathcal{H}=\frac{1}{2m_{1}}\|\mathbf{p}_{1}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2m_{2}}\|\mathbf{p}_{2}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2m_{3}}\|\mathbf{p}_{3}\|^{2}-\frac{m_{1}m_{2}}{\|\mathbf{q}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{2}\|}-\frac{m_{1}m_{3}}{\|\mathbf{q}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{3}\|}-\frac{m_{2}m_{3}}{\|\mathbf{q}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{3}\|},

(67)

with
m_{i}
the mass of body
i
,
\mathbf{p}_{i}
the momentum of body
i
, and
\mathbf{q}_{i}
the position of body
i
. Denote by
\mathbf{q}
the vector
\left(\mathbf{q}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}},\mathbf{q}_{2}^{\mathsf{T}},\mathbf{q}_{3}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{\mathsf{T}}
with similar notation for
\mathbf{p}
. We consider estimating the Hamiltonian (
67
) directly with a physically motivated overparameterized basis

\hat{\mathcal{H}}(\hat{\mathbf{a}})=\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{p})\hat{\mathbf{a}}

to form the dynamics predictor (
28
) & (
29
), and choose
\psi(\cdot)=\frac{1}{2}\|\cdot\|_{1.05}^{2}
to promote sparsity. Further simulation details are given in Appendix
B

Results are shown in Figure
5
. Figure
5
A displays convergence of the predictor trajectory
\hat{\mathbf{x}}(t)
(solid) to the true trajectory
\mathbf{x}(t)
(open circles). The simulation is run open-loop and all parameters with magnitude less than
10^{-3}
are set equal to zero past
t=10
, indicated with a vertical line. The open-loop predictor maintains the correct oscillatory behavior. Figure
5
B displays convergence of
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}(t)
to zero with adaptation (top) and demonstrates that adaptation is necessary for convergence (bottom). When switching to the open-loop predictor past
t=10
, the system without adaptation sustains large errors, while the learned predictor maintains good performance. Figure
5
C shows the qualitative structure of the predictor trajectory without learning (solid): when run closed-loop, it does not converge to the true trajectory (open circles), and when run open-loop, it spuriously converges to a fixed point. Figures
5
D and E show parameter trajectories and asymptotically converged parameters, respectively. Together, the two panes demonstrate that the implicit bias of the algorithm ensures convergence to a relatively sparse estimate of the system Hamiltonian.

\put(5.0,60.0){{A}}
\end{overpic}

\put(5.0,60.0){{B}}
\end{overpic}

\end{overpic}

Three-body system.
(A) Convergence of the predictor with adaptation (solid) to the true system trajectory (open circles). Past the vertical line at
t=10
, coefficients below
10^{-3}
are removed and the predictor is run open-loop, demonstrating that the limit cycle is correctly learned. (B) Predictor error
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}=\hat{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{x}
for the adaptive dynamics predictor (
28
) & (
29
) with adaptation (top) and without adaptation (bottom). The system without adaptation is inaccurate when run open-loop, while the system with adaptation maintains accuracy. (C) Failure of the predictor trajectory without adaptation (solid) to converge to the true system trajectory (open circles). When run open-loop, the predictor without learning incorrectly tends to a fixed point. (D) Parameter trajectories for the adaptive dynamics predictor. Many parameters stay at or near zero, as predicted by Proposition
3.1
. (E) Histogram of final parameter values learned by the adaptive dynamics predictor.

9.4
Sparse identification of chemical reaction networks

Consider a set of chemical reactions with
N
distinct chemical species. Under the continuum hypothesis and the well-mixed assumption, mass-action kinetics dictates that the system dynamics can be described exactly in a monomial basis
(
Liu et al. 2013
)
,

\displaystyle\nu_{j}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=k_{j}\Pi_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}^{a_{ji}},

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{x}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\nu}(\mathbf{x}),

where
x_{i}
is the concentration of chemical species
i
,
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
is the stoichiometric matrix, and the
a_{ji}
are stoichiometric coefficients. Under the assumption that the full state of the network is measured, consider the adaptive dynamics predictor

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{x}}}

\displaystyle=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\hat{\mathbf{x}})+k\left(\mathbf{x}-\hat{\mathbf{x}}\right),

(68)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\nabla\psi\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\gamma(\hat{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{x})\hat{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\hat{\mathbf{x}})^{\mathsf{T}},

(69)

with
\gamma>0
a positive learning rate,
k>0
a measurement gain,
\psi
a strongly convex function,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}
an estimate of the stoichiometric matrix, and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\hat{\mathbf{x}})
a vector of monomial basis functions representing available knowledge of the system. Here we consider a four-species chemical reaction network (cf.
Liu et al. 2013
, supplementary information),

\displaystyle\dot{x}_{1}

\displaystyle=-k_{1}x_{1}x_{2},

\displaystyle\dot{x}_{2}

\displaystyle=-k_{1}x_{1}x_{2}-k_{2}x_{2}x_{3}^{2},

\displaystyle\dot{x}_{3}

\displaystyle=-k_{1}x_{1}x_{2}-2k_{2}x_{2}x_{3}^{2},

\displaystyle\dot{x}_{4}

\displaystyle=k_{2}x_{2}x_{3}^{2},

with corresponding adaptive dynamics predictor (
68
) & (
69
). We set
\hat{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
to be a vector of all monomials up to degree three comprising a total of
140
candidate basis functions, and we set
\psi(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}})=\frac{1}{2}\left\|\mathsf{vec}\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}\right)\right\|_{1.01}^{2}
to identify a sparse, parsimonious model consistent with the data. Searching over sparse models ensures that our learned predictor selects only a few relevant terms in the approximate system dynamics. We fix
k=1.5
and
\gamma=0.25
for
t<10
. As in Section
9.3
, past
t=10
we set
k=\gamma=0
and run the predictor open-loop. We also perform shrinkage and set all coefficients with magnitude below
10^{-3}
formally equal to zero, leaving
19
remaining parameters.

Results are shown in Figure
6
. In Figure
6
A, we show convergence of the observer error to zero with adaptation (solid) and divergence away from zero without adaptation (dashed), demonstrating that adaptation is necessary for effective prediction. The inset displays a closer look at the asymptotic behavior of the open-loop dynamics predictor after shrinkage, which shows that the fixed point of the system is correctly learned. Figure
6
B shows convergence of
\hat{\mathbf{x}}(t)
(solid) to
\mathbf{x}(t)
(dashed). Figure
6
C displays parameter trajectories as a function of time. Many parameters stay at or near zero as predicted by Proposition
3.1
; use of a Euclidean law would lead to a Gaussian-like spread over parameter vectors as in Figure
2
C. The inset displays a finer-grained view around zero of the parameter trajectories. Figure
6
D shows a histogram of the final parameter values learned by the adaptive dynamics predictor, demonstrating that only a few relevant terms are identified.

\begin{overpic}[chem_shrink_ol_new/xtilde.pdf]
\put(5.0,60.0){{A}}
\end{overpic}

\begin{overpic}[chem_shrink_ol_new/state.pdf]
\put(5.0,60.0){{B}}
\end{overpic}

\begin{overpic}[chem_shrink_ol_new/params.pdf]
\put(5.0,60.0){{C}}
\end{overpic}

\begin{overpic}[chem_shrink_ol_new/hist.pdf]
\put(5.0,60.0){{D}}
\end{overpic}

Figure 6
:
Chemical reaction network.
(A) Observer error
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}=\hat{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{x}
for the adaptive dynamics predictor (
68
) & (
69
) with adaptation (solid) and without adaptation (dashed). The predictor without adaptation diverges immediately. Past
t=10
, shrinkage is performed, all coefficients with magnitude below
10^{-3}
are set to zero, and the predictor is run open-loop with
k=\gamma=0
. (B) Convergence of
\hat{\mathbf{x}}
to
\mathbf{x}
for the adaptive dynamics predictor. The predictor accurately learns the fixed-point of the system and stays stationary when run open-loop. (C) Parameter trajectories for the adaptive dynamics predictor. Many parameters stay at or near zero, as predicted by Proposition
3.1
. (D) Histogram of final parameter values learned by the adaptive dynamics predictor. Only a few relevant terms are identified.

10
Concluding remarks and future directions

It is somewhat unusual in nonlinear control to have a choice between a large variety of algorithms that can all be proven to globally converge. Nevertheless, in this paper, we have presented a large set of new globally convergent adaptive control algorithms and analyzed their implicit bias properties. The general philosophy behind the development of these new algorithms is to leverage insight from recent developments in optimization and machine learning. As such, the results in this paper strengthen existing parallels between adaptive control, machine learning, and online optimization.

The new algorithms combine the velocity gradient methodology
(
Fradkov 1980
;
Fradkov et al. 1999
)
with the Bregman Lagrangian
(
Wibisono et al. 2016
;
Betancourt et al. 2018
)
to systematically generate velocity gradient algorithms with momentum. These algorithms can be seen as an adaptive control analogue of Nesterov’s method, and admit an alternative interpretation as adding a form of Polyak-Ruppert averaging to classical first-order methods. Based on this latter analogy, using a parallel to distributed stochastic gradient descent algorithms
(
Zhang et al. 2014
;
Boffi and Slotine 2020
)
, we developed a stable modification of our algorithms that consists of feedback coupling with an exponentially weighted average.

Throughout the paper, for simplicity of exposition, we gave concrete examples using the
n^{\text{th}}
order system (
1
). As discussed in Remark
2.2
, our results extend to more general systems that have an error model similar to (
6
).

The most promising direction of future research is to understand when and whether or not there is an empirical advantage to using our proposed momentum algorithms. In optimization, accelerated algorithms generated by the Bregman Lagrangian provide faster convergence when properly discretized, and it is thus likely that a careful discretization is necessary to obtain optimal performance of our momentum algorithms. However, it is not possible to obtain finite-time convergence rates in adaptive control, and it would be necessary to prove such rates to understand analytically if there is an advantage. The analogy with Polyak-Ruppert averaging suggests that the momentum algorithms and the algorithms of Section
7
may be helpful in the presence of noise; here, the most likely angle of attack would be a stochastic discrete-time analysis.

Similar higher-order algorithms have appeared in the literature for adaptive control of linear systems of relative degree greater than one
(
Morse 1992
)
, where first-order adaptation laws provably cannot control the system. Here we have focused on feedback linearizable systems, and perhaps there exist classes of nonlinear systems that cannot be adaptively controlled with a first-order algorithm, but can with a momentum algorithm. We leave the investigation of these interesting and important questions to future work.

Appendix A
Simulation details

A.1
Further simulation details for Section
9.1

The PI form of the non-Euclidean variant of algorithm (
58
) is given by

\displaystyle\nabla\psi(\hat{\mathbf{v}})

\displaystyle=\overline{v}+\xi(\mathbf{x},t)+\boldsymbol{\rho}(\mathbf{x},t),

\displaystyle\xi(\mathbf{x},t)

\displaystyle=-\gamma s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))\tanh(\mathbf{V}\mathbf{x}),

\displaystyle\rho(\mathbf{x},t)

\displaystyle=\gamma\left[\tanh\left(\mathbf{V}\mathbf{x}\right)x_{2}-\log\left(\cosh\left(\mathbf{V}\mathbf{x}\right)\right)\oslash\mathbf{V}_{2}+\left(\lambda\tilde{x}-x_{2,d}(t)\right)\tanh\left(\mathbf{V}\mathbf{x}\right)\right],

\displaystyle\dot{\overline{v}}

\displaystyle=\gamma\left(\dot{x}_{2,d}(t)-\lambda\left(x_{2}-x_{2,d}(t)\right)-\eta s\right)\tanh(\mathbf{V}\mathbf{x})+\gamma x_{2}\tanh(\mathbf{V}\mathbf{x})\circ\mathbf{V}_{1}\oslash\mathbf{V}_{2},

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}

\displaystyle=\beta\left(1+\mu\|\tanh(\mathbf{V}\mathbf{x})\|^{2}\right)\left(\hat{\mathbf{v}}-\hat{\mathbf{a}}\right),

where
\circ
and
\oslash
denote elementwise multiplication and division respectively, where
\mathbf{V}_{i}
is the
i^{\text{th}}
column of
\mathbf{V}
, and where
\hat{\mathbf{v}}
is obtained from
\nabla\psi(\hat{\mathbf{v}})
by inverting
\nabla\psi
. For the squared
p
norm
\psi(\cdot)=\frac{1}{2}\|\cdot\|_{p}^{2}
, the inverse function can be analytically computed as

\left(\nabla\psi^{-1}\right)(\mathbf{y})=\|\mathbf{y}\|_{q}^{2-q}|\mathbf{y}|^{q-1}\text{sign}(\mathbf{y})

(70)

where
\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{p}=1
,
|\cdot|
denotes elementwise absolute value and
\text{sign}(\cdot)
denotes elementwise
sign

(
Gentile 2003
)
. We consider the
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{4},\ell_{6}
, and
\ell_{10}
norms for
\psi
. To approximate the
\ell_{1}
norm, (
70
) is used with
p=1.1
. All other
p
norms can be used directly.

In all simulations we take
\lambda=.5
in the definition of
s
(
4
) and
\eta=.5
in the control input (
5
). For the adaptation hyperparameters, we choose
\gamma=1.5
for the
\ell_{2},\ell_{4}
, and
\ell_{6}
norms. We take
\gamma=50
for the
\ell_{1}
norm and
\gamma=.5
for the
\ell_{10}
norm
8
8

8

These values of
\gamma
were chosen to ensure good control performance without excessively high control inputs or fast parameter adaptation. In particular, adaptation occurs very slowly with
\ell_{1}
regularization, as small parameters are quickly eliminated to promote sparsity. A high adaptation gain was needed to ensure adaptation on a similar timescale to the other norms.
. In all cases,
\beta=1
and
\mu=\frac{3\gamma}{2\eta\beta}
. We set
\dim(\mathbf{a})=\dim(\hat{\mathbf{a}})=500
and randomly initialize
\hat{\mathbf{a}}
and
\hat{\mathbf{v}}
around zero from a normal distribution with standard deviation
10^{-3}
. The true parameter vector
\mathbf{a}
is drawn from a normal distribution with mean zero and standard deviation
7.5
. The matrix
\mathbf{V}
is set to have normally distributed elements with standard deviation
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\dim{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}}}
. The state vector is initialized such that
\mathbf{x}(0)=\mathbf{x}_{d}(0)
. The desired trajectory is taken to be

x_{d}(t)=\sin\left(\frac{\sqrt{2}\pi}{12}t+\cos\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}\pi}{12}t\right)\right).

A.2
Further simulation details for Section
9.2

To define the control primitives, we consider a distribution over tasks specified by random desired trajectories

x_{d}^{(i)}(t)=M\sin(A_{i}t+B_{i}\cos(C_{i}t))+D_{i}

with
D_{i}=2i(-1)^{i}\times M
,
A_{i}\sim\mathsf{Unif}(0,5\pi)
,
B_{i}\sim\mathsf{Unif}(0,3)
, and
C_{i}\sim\mathsf{Unif}(0,5\pi)
. The shift
D_{i}
ensures that the desired trajectories occupy non-overlapping regions of state space. We then learn primitives
\left\{u_{i}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}}^{(i)})\right\}_{i=1}^{N}
to track
\left\{x_{d}^{(i)}(t)\right\}_{i=1}^{N}
where each
u_{i}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}}^{(i)})
is given by (
5
) with parameter estimates
\hat{\mathbf{a}}^{(i)}
. The parameter estimates are found via the Slotine and Li adaptation law

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}^{(i)}

\displaystyle=-\gamma\tanh(\mathbf{V}\mathbf{x})^{\mathsf{T}}s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t)),

which is allowed to run until the parameter estimates converge. We set
p=15
,
N=300
,
M=0.1
,
\gamma=5
, and
\eta=\lambda=0.5
. Each vector of parameter estimates
\hat{\mathbf{a}}^{(i)}
is initialized randomly,
\hat{\mathbf{a}}^{(i)}(0)\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\sigma^{2}_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}\right)
with
\sigma_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}=10^{-3}
. The state is initialized randomly for each task,
\mathbf{x}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\sigma_{\mathbf{x}}^{2}\right)
with
\sigma_{\mathbf{x}}=5
. The true parameters
\mathbf{a}
are drawn randomly,
\mathbf{a}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\sigma_{\mathbf{a}}^{2}\right)
with
\sigma_{\mathbf{a}}=2
.

We then fix a time horizon
T
and a number of tasks
k
, and set the desired trajectory in the experiment to be

x_{d}(t)=x_{d}^{(i_{l})}(t)\text{ if }t_{l-1}\leq t<t_{l},

with
l=1,\ldots,k
,
i_{l}
drawn uniformly from
i=1,\ldots,N
,
t_{0}=0
, and
t_{l}=\frac{lT}{k}
. The above desired trajectory switches between desired trajectories
x_{d}^{(i_{l})}
that
u_{i_{l}}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}}^{(i_{l})})
has learned to track. In the experiment, we fix
T=1000
and set
k=5
.

To perform the experiment, we use the
lsoda
integrator in
scipy.integrate.ode
. For the Euclidean adaptation law, we zero any components if they become negative and project onto the simplex. For the non-Euclidean adaptation law, we integrate the mirror descent-like dynamics directly, and update
\nabla\psi\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right)
via
lsoda
. After each timestep, we compute
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
by inverting the gradient of
\psi
, which ensures that each component
\hat{\beta}_{i}\geq 0
. We then project onto the simplex. In both cases, the projection is performed by dividing by the
\ell_{1}
norm of the parameter vector,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mapsto\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}/\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|_{1}
.

Appendix B
Further simulation details for Section
9.3

We define
\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{p})\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times 21}
to be a row vector of basis functions consisting of quadratics and quartics in
\mathbf{p}_{i}
and
\mathbf{q}_{i}
, as well as
1/r_{ij}
,
1/r_{ij}^{2}
, and
1/r_{ij}^{3}
potentials with
r_{ij}=\|\mathbf{q}_{i}-\mathbf{q}_{j}\|
for
i\neq j
, comprising
21
total basis functions. These choices represent standard expressions for kinetic energy, spring potentials, central force potentials, and higher-order terms; any basis functions can be chosen motivated by knowledge of the physical system at hand.

We set
k=5
,
\gamma=3.5
, and choose
\psi(\cdot)=\frac{1}{2}\|\cdot\|_{1.05}^{2}
to identify basis functions relevant to the observed trajectory. We fix
m_{i}=1
for all
i
and initialize
\mathbf{q}
and
\mathbf{p}
to lock the system in an oscillatory mode. Past
t=10
, we set
k=\gamma=0
and run the predictor open-loop, as well as perform shrinkage and set all coefficients with magnitude below
10^{-3}
formally equal to zero, leaving
13
remaining terms.

Appendix C
Background results

Barbalat’s Lemma is a classical technique in adaptive control theory, which is used in conjuction with a Lyapunov-like analysis to prove convergence of a given signal.

Lemma C.1

(Barbalat’s Lemma,
(
Slotine and Li 1991
)
)
.

Assume that
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{t}|x(\tau)|d\tau<\infty
. If
x(t)
is uniformly continuous, then
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}x(t)=0
.

A sufficient condition for uniform continuity of
x(t)
is for
\dot{x}(t)
to be bounded. It follows that for any signal
x(t)\in\mathcal{L}_{2}\cap\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
with
\dot{x}(t)\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
, we can apply Lemma
C.1
to the signal
x^{2}(t)
and conclude that
x(t)\rightarrow 0
.

Lemma C.2
.

Assume that
\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{f}^{2}(\mathbf{x}(t^{\prime}),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t^{\prime}),\mathbf{a},t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}<\infty
where
[0,T)
is the maximal interval of existence of
\mathbf{x}(t)
. Further assume that
\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t)
is bounded over
[0,T)
, that both bounds are independent of
T
, and that
\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},\mathbf{a},t)
is locally bounded in
\mathbf{x}
and
\hat{\mathbf{a}}
uniformly in
t
. Then
\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
,
\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x}(\cdot),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\cdot),\mathbf{a},\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}_{2}
,
s(\mathbf{x}(\cdot),\mathbf{x}_{d}(\cdot))\in\mathcal{L}_{2}\cap\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
,
s(\mathbf{x}(t),\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))\rightarrow 0
and
\mathbf{x}(t)\rightarrow\mathbf{x}_{d}(t)
.

Proof.

By (
6
), we can write explicitly

s(\mathbf{x}(t),\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\eta(t-\tau)}\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x}(\tau),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\tau),\mathbf{a},\tau)d\tau.

(71)

By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle s^{2}(\mathbf{x}(T),\mathbf{x}_{d}(T))

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2\eta(T-\tau)}d\tau\right)\left(\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{f}^{2}(\mathbf{x}(\tau),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\tau),\mathbf{a},\tau)d\tau\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\eta}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{f}^{2}(\mathbf{x}(\tau),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\tau),\mathbf{a},\tau)d\tau\right)\left(1-e^{-2\eta T}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\eta}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{f}^{2}(\mathbf{x}(\tau),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\tau),\mathbf{a},\tau)d\tau\right)<\infty

so that
\sup_{t\in[0,T)}|s(\mathbf{x}(t),\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))|<\infty
. Observe that this bound is independent of
T
. It immediately follows that
\sup_{t\in[0,T)}\|\mathbf{x}(t)\|<\infty
, and that this bound is independent of
T
. This observation contradicts that
[0,T)
is the maximal interval of existence of
\mathbf{x}(t)
for any
T
, and thus
\mathbf{x}(t)
must exist for all
t
. This shows that
\mathbf{x}(\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
,
s(\mathbf{x}(\cdot),\mathbf{x}_{d}(\cdot))\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
, and that the bounds on
\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x}(t),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t),\mathbf{a},t)
and
\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t)
for
t\in[0,T)
can be extended for all
t
. From this we conclude
\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x}(\cdot),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\cdot),\mathbf{a},\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}_{2}
and
\hat{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
. Similarly, Parseval’s theorem applied to the low-pass filter (
71
) shows that
s(\mathbf{x}(\cdot),\mathbf{x}_{d}(\cdot))\in\mathcal{L}_{2}
. Because
\mathbf{x}(\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
and
\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
, and because
\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},\mathbf{a},t)
is locally bounded in
\mathbf{x}
and
\hat{\mathbf{a}}
uniformly in
t
,
\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x}(\cdot),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\cdot),\mathbf{a},\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
. By (
6
),
\dot{s}(\mathbf{x}(\cdot),\mathbf{x}_{d}(\cdot),\dot{\mathbf{x}}(\cdot),\dot{\mathbf{x}}_{d}(\cdot))\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
, and hence by Lemma
C.1
,
s(\mathbf{x}(t),\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))\rightarrow 0
. By definition of
s
, we then conclude that
\mathbf{x}(t)\rightarrow\mathbf{x}_{d}(t)
.
∎

Appendix D
Generalized linear models

Generalized linear model (GLM) regression is an extension of linear regression where the data is assumed to be generated by a function of the form
f(\mathbf{x})=u(\mathbf{w}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{x})
for a known “link function”
u
and unknown parameters
\mathbf{w}
. The first computationally and statistically efficient algorithm for this problem – the GLM-Tron of
Kakade et al. 2011
– assumes that
u
is Lipschitz and monotonic, much like Assumption
2.2
. The GLM-Tron algorithm was recently extended to the setting of kernel methods, and was subsequently used to provably learn two hidden layer neural networks by
Goel and Klivans 2017
; this extension is known as the Alphatron. In the kernel GLM setting handled by the Alphatron, the function to be approximated is assumed to be of the form
f(\mathbf{x})=u\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{K}\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{i}\right)w_{i}\right)
where
\mathcal{K}
is the kernel function for a Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Space (RKHS)
\mathcal{H}
.

The Alphatron initializes all weights to zero, and given a batch of labeled training data
\left(\mathbf{x}_{i},f(\mathbf{x}_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{m}
, updates with a learning rate
\lambda>0
according to the iteration

\hat{w}_{i}^{t+1}=\hat{w}_{i}^{t}-\frac{\lambda}{m}\left(\hat{f}(\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{t},\mathbf{x}_{i})-f(\mathbf{x}_{i})\right).

(72)

We now demonstrate an equivalence between Tyukin’s adaptation law (
12
) and the Alphatron weight update (
72
) in the following lemma.

Lemma D.1
.

The adaptation law (
12
) is an application of the Alphatron algorithm (
72
) to adaptive control.

The proof is given in Appendix
G.2
.

Lemma
D.1
shows a convergence of techniques in nonlinearly parameterized adaptive control and provably correct learning algorithms with associated nonconvex optimization problems. It suggests that models amenable to gradient-based optimization in machine learning are good targets for nonlinear parameterizations suitable for adaptive control.

Appendix E
Exponential forgetting least squares and bounded gain forgetting

We now apply the exponential forgetting and bounded gain forgetting least squares techniques (cf. Chapter 9 of
Slotine and Li 1991
) to the algorithms developed in this work. These techniques are useful for estimation of time-varying parameters, as they rapidly discard previous information used for parameter estimation. Exponential forgetting least squares is described by a time-dependent learning rate matrix
\mathbf{P}(t)
, which, in the linearly parameterized case
\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},\mathbf{a},t)=\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)\tilde{\mathbf{a}}
takes the form

\dot{\mathbf{P}}=\begin{cases}\lambda\mathbf{P}-\mathbf{P}\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x}(t),t)^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x}(t),t)\mathbf{P}&\text{if\ \ }\|\mathbf{P}\|\leq P_{0}\\
0&\text{else}\end{cases}

(73)

where
\lambda>0
is a constant forgetting factor,
P_{0}
is a maximum bound on the norm, and
\|\mathbf{P}\|
is a matrix norm such as the opeator norm. Equation (
73
) implies for the inverse matrix

\frac{d}{dt}\mathbf{P}^{-1}=\begin{cases}-\lambda\mathbf{P}^{-1}+\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x}(t),t)^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x}(t),t)&\text{if\ \ }\|\mathbf{P}\|\leq P_{0}\\
0&\text{else}\end{cases}

(74)

In the nonlinearly parameterized case described by Assumption
2.2
, we will replace
\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x}(t),t)^{\mathsf{T}}
in (
73
) & (
74
) by
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\mathbf{x}(t),t)
. In the bounded gain forgetting technique,
\lambda
is a time-dependent function

\lambda(t)=\lambda_{0}\left(1-\frac{\|\mathbf{P}\|}{P_{0}}\right),

(75)

where
\lambda_{0}>0
sets the forgetting factor when the norm of
\mathbf{P}
is small. It can be shown that this choice of
\lambda(t)
ensures that
\|\mathbf{P}\|\leq P_{0}
, and thus we may drop the case statement in (
73
) & (
74
)
(
Slotine and Li 1991
)
. The choice of
\lambda(t)
in bounded gain forgetting and the case statement used in (
73
) & (
74
) are both employed to prevent unboundedness of the learning rate matrix.

We focus on algorithms without the elastic modification of Section
7
; extension to the elastic modification is simple. We also focus on the bounded gain forgetting technique: proofs for the exponential forgetting least squares technique are identical, with the addition of an appropriate case statement in the time derivative of the Lyapunov function. For simplicity, we include only the time-dependent gain
\mathbf{P}(t)
and set the scalar gains
\kappa=\gamma=1
where applicable.

We begin with the first-order non-filtered composite (
49
) with
\mathbf{P}
given by (
73
). In this case, the composite algorithm may be implemented via the PI form (
50
)-(
6.2
) where now
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}(t)
.

Proposition E.1
.

Consider the adaptation algorithm (
49
) with
\mathbf{P}(t)
given by (
73
),
\lambda(t)
given by (
75
), and
\kappa=\gamma=1
. Then all trajectories
(\mathbf{x}(t),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t))
remain bounded,
\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x}(\cdot),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\cdot),\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}_{2}\cap\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
,
s(\mathbf{x}(\cdot),\mathbf{x}_{d}(\cdot))\in\mathcal{L}_{2}\cap\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
,
s(\mathbf{x}(t),\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))\rightarrow 0
and
\mathbf{x}(t)\rightarrow\mathbf{x}_{d}(t)
.

The proof is given in Appendix
G.3

We can state a similar result for the higher-order non-filtered composite with time-dependent
\mathbf{P}(t)
given by (
73
),

\ddot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}+\left(\beta\mathcal{N}(t)-\frac{\dot{\mathcal{N}}(t)}{\mathcal{N}(t)}-\dot{\mathbf{P}}(t)\mathbf{P}^{-1}(t)\right)\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}=-\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\mathbf{P}(t)\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)^{\mathsf{T}}\left(s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))+\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},\mathbf{a},t)\right),

(76)

which admits a representation as two first-order equations,

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{v}}}

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{P}(t)\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x},t)^{\mathsf{T}}\left(s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))+\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},\mathbf{a},t)\right),

(77)

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}

\displaystyle=\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\mathbf{P}(t)\left(\hat{\mathbf{v}}-\hat{\mathbf{a}}\right).

(78)

Equation (
77
) can be implemented via the PI form
\hat{\mathbf{v}}=\overline{\mathbf{v}}+\boldsymbol{\xi}(\mathbf{x},t)+\boldsymbol{\rho}(\mathbf{x},t)
where
\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\rho}
, and
\dot{\overline{\mathbf{v}}}
are given by (
50
), (
51
), and (
6.2
) respectively with
\gamma=\kappa=1
.

Proposition E.2
.

Consider the adaptation algorithm (
76
) with
\mathbf{P}(t)
given by (
73
),
\lambda(t)
given by (
75
),
\mathcal{N}(t)=1+\mu\|\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x}(t),t)\|^{2}
and
\mu>\frac{3\eta+2}{2\eta\beta}
. Then all trajectories
(\mathbf{x}(\cdot),\hat{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\cdot))
remain bounded,
\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x}(\cdot),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\cdot),\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}_{2}\cap\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
,
s(\mathbf{x}(\cdot),\mathbf{x}_{d}(\cdot))\in\mathcal{L}_{2}\cap\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
,
s(\mathbf{x}(t),\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))\rightarrow 0
and
\mathbf{x}(t)\rightarrow\mathbf{x}_{d}(t)
.

The proof is given in Appendix
G.4
.

Remark E.1
.

Because
\mathbf{P}(t)
is uniformly bounded in
t
, it is not necessary to include
\mathbf{P}(t)
in (
78
); by a slight modification of the proof, it is easy to show that the modified higher-order law

\ddot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}+\left(\beta\mathcal{N}(t)-\frac{\dot{\mathcal{N}}(t)}{\mathcal{N}(t)}\right)\dot{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}=-\beta\mathcal{N}(t)\mathbf{P}(t)\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{x}(t),t)^{\mathsf{T}}\left(s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))+\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},\mathbf{a},t)\right),

is also a stable adaptive law with a suitable choice of gains.
\ \ \diamond

We now consider Tyukin’s first-order algorithm for nonlinearly parameterized systems (
12
) with
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}(t)
given by (
73
). To do so, we require an additional assumption

Assumption E.1
.

For the same function
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\mathbf{x},t)
as in Assumption
2.2
, there exists a constant
D_{2}
such that

|\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},\mathbf{a},t)|\geq D_{2}|\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\mathbf{x},t)^{\mathsf{T}}\tilde{\mathbf{a}}|.

(79)

Together with Assumption
2.2
, Assumption
E.1
states that
\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},\mathbf{a},t)
lies between two linear functions. Given that the update (
73
) is derived based on recursive
linear
least squares considerations, it is unsurprising that such an assumption is required in the nonlinearly parameterized setting. We are now in a position to state the following proposition.

Proposition E.3
.

Consider the adaptation algorithm (
12
) with
\mathbf{P}(t)
given by (
73
) and
\lambda(t)
given by (
75
) for
\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{a}},\mathbf{a},t)
satisfying Assumptions
2.2
and
E.1
. Further assume that
D_{1}<2D_{2}^{2}
or that
D_{2}>\frac{1}{2}
. Then, all trajectories
(\mathbf{x}(t),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t))
remain bounded,
\tilde{f}(\mathbf{x}(\cdot),\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\cdot),\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}_{2}
,
s(\mathbf{x}(\cdot),\mathbf{x}_{d}(\cdot))\in\mathcal{L}_{2}\cap\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
,
s(\mathbf{x}(t),\mathbf{x}_{d}(t))\rightarrow 0
and
